                                                                7.0 RESILIENCY

                                              INTRODUCTION
                                              Currently, resiliency is not addressed in Bluffdale’s previous general plan or
                                              city policies. Creating a resilient future benefits its citizens and is reflected
                                              throughout the community. To create such a community, an understanding and
                                              support for the value of resiliency at a municipal level is needed.



                                              7.1 EXISTING CONDITIONS
                                              Resiliency is defined as the ability to meet the needs of the current generation
                                              without compromising the ability for future generations to meet their needs.

                    Natural                   Resiliency is a broad term that covers three different pillars: the economic
                                              resiliency for the municipality, the social landscape, and the natural health within
                                              the city and the greater region. Resiliency occurs at the intersection of the three
                                              pillars. A change in community health may affect economic resiliency, or may
         Social                Economic
                                              be impacted by an natural change. Natural resources within the city include
                                              the ecology, air quality, and water quality. Resiliency matters are integrated
                                              into many other sections of the General Plan including Land Use, Economic
Figure 7.1 Resilient development within the   Development, Housing, and Open Space + Trails.
City includes social, natural, and economic
components.                                   Committing to creating a resilient future for itself and its citizens, and it being
                                              reflected throughout the community, will be a benefit to all. The City may want
                                              to invest in each of the pillars to focus attention towards achieving the attainable
                                              and implementable strategies within this document and within each of the city’s
                                              departments.




                                                                                                           7.0 Resiliency    60
                                                                                                          BLUFFDALE GENERAL PLAN




                                                    7.2 FUTURE CONSIDERATIONS
                                                    ECONOMIC RESILIENCY
                                                    Economic Resiliency means long-term economic growth without negative
                                                    impacts on social, natural and cultural aspects of a community. A resilient
                                                    economy ensures that resources, such as land, labor and capital are managed
                                                    fairly and to benefit society. It also uses resources efficiently and responsibly so it
                                                    can operate successfully for future generations.

                                                    Resilient economies are also diverse, have equal opportunities, and advance the
                                                    individuals within them. Bluffdale has several advantages in that it has a wide
                                                    mix of business types and land uses, and a wide range of price points for land
Figure 7.2 - Economic resiliency includes           and buildings. In short, its diversity means there are opportunities for many, and
diversifying the tax base and supporting job
                                                    room for growth.
growth.
                                                    Other ways in which economic resiliency is exemplified include:
                                                    1. Diversification of tax base
                                                    2. Planning for future city investments
                                                    3. Supporting job growth
                                                    4. Access to opportunity

                                                    NATURAL RESILIENCY
                                                    Natural Resiliency makes it possible for present day needs to be met without
                                                    compromising the ability of future generations to do the same. Many factors
                                                    contribute to the natural resiliency of a community, but incremental changes in
                                                    municipal policy and incentives can influence resiliency on a community as well
                                                    as household scale.

                                                    Moving towards Natural Resiliency is a common goal among all community
                                                    leaders, citizens, employers, and other organizations. Natural systems such
                                                    as watersheds, soils, landforms, wind, and air masses transcend political
Figure 7.3 - Many city projects and policies have   boundaries and affect regions larger than the boundaries of any one jurisdiction.
an overlapping resiliency motivation, and can be    Collaboration between cities and regions is essential. A coalition based on
both supportive of natural and economic systems.
                                                    bio-regions would focus on natural systems rather than political boundaries for
                                                    building effective and resilient policies.

                                                    The city’s Natural Resiliency toolbox should include programs that address water
                                                    conservation, recycling, energy efficiency, active transportation, and open space
                                                    preservation (Figure 7.3).




 APRIL 22 DRAFT                                                                                                    7.0 Resiliency     61
                                                                                                                    BLUFFDALE GENERAL PLAN



                                                       WATER CONSERVATION

                                                   The City of Bluffdale adopted the 2021 Water Conservation Plan Update in
                                                   December, 2021. The City recognizes the need for proactive planning to meet
                                                   the water needs of its citizens. The Utah State Legislature has passed legislation
                                                   requiring public water suppliers to prepare a Water Conservation Plan and
                                                   then to update the plan periodically. The 2021 report is an update of the City’s
                                                   2015 Water Conservation Plan. This report describes the drinking water system,
                                                   summarizes water consumption, assesses the water conservation alternatives
                                                   available to the City, sets goals to conserve water, and identifies existing and
                                                   proposed water conservation measures to be implemented by the City. The plan
                                                   in its entirety can be found in the appendix of this general plan document.

                                                       Goal

                                                   The new Regional Municipal and Industrial 2030 Water Conservation Goal for the
                                                   Salt Lake region is to reduce consumption by 11% from the year 2015 to 2030,
Figure 7.4 - Bluffdale’s 2021 Water Conservation   resulting in a reduction goal of 210 to 187 gallons per capita per day (gpcd).
Plan Update, December 2021.                        Bluffdale City is already below the regional goal, and therefore, has the goal to
                                                   also have an 11% reduction from 2015 to 2030, following the same trajectory as
                                                   the regional goal. The City was at 171 gpcd in 2015. The City’s goal is to be at 152
                                                   gpcd by 2030.1

                                                   SOCIAL RESILIENCY
                                                   Civic infrastructure includes the organizations and relationships that people
                                                   use to solve problems, build community, and be resilient, whereby advancing
                                                   Social Resiliency. A sense of community and belonging has a proven impact on
                                                   public health, safety and welfare. Recreational programming in Bluffdale such
                                                   can use public spaces to build relationships and engage people in community
                                                   conversations. Social Resiliency includes:
                                                   1. Building community culture using placemaking strategies
                                                   2. Community pride
                                                   3. Public engagement with city decisions
                                                   4. Neighborhood support and problem solving

                                                   City events and programs that are offered for free or very low cost, appeal to the
                                                   changing needs of the culturally diverse and growing youth population. Even
                                                   as the community welcomes many new residents, there is also a proud group of
                                                   life-long legacy residents who provide stability and identity to Bluffdale.

                                                   The city’s Social Resiliency toolbox could include:
                                                   1. More parks, trails and open spaces with amenities and activities/events that
                                                      bring people together.
                                                   2. Expansion of a civic center in Bluffdale around City Hall.
                                                   1   Source: City Of Bluffdale 2021 Water Conservation Plan Update, Hansen, Allen & Luce Engineers


  APRIL 22 DRAFT                                                                                                               7.0 Resiliency          62
                                                                                                              BLUFFDALE GENERAL PLAN



                                                           3. Placemaking projects, such as wayfinding, public art, and pop-up public
                                                              space, to bring people together in new and different ways.
                                                           4. Expanded outreach tools, including technology-based communications
                                                              to simplify interactions with city government and increase community
                                                              engagement.
                                                           5. More diverse programs at all community gathering spaces, including
                                                              recreation, education, arts, senior programs, job and career tools, food and
                                                              hygiene pantries, and digital access.
                                                           6. Events that reflect the community and its interests (Figure 7.5).

expansion of social connections and greater                7. Continued opportunities to volunteer and get involved in civic activities and
engagement with neighbors.
                                                              human services.

                                                           Goals
                                                           1. Support preservation of natural features that contribute to the character of
                                                              Bluffdale.
                                                           2. Encourage city policies that support economic and fiscal resiliency.
                                                           3. Support social resiliency by promoting policies that encourage community
                                                              engagement and public participation.
Figure 7.6 - A first step to resiliency for Bluffdale is
to form a working group to begin to define how
community values should influence the city’s               Policies
policies on resiliency.                                    1. Identify the City’s resiliency priorities.
                                                           2. Establish a resiliency working group (Figure 7.6).
                                                           3. Engage residents and staff in the resiliency discussion by initiating an
                                                              education and awareness effort.

                                                           Implementation Strategies
                                                           1. Coordinate with other municipalities and entities on policies that support
                                                              resiliency (recycling, water usage, active transportation, land use, economic
                                                              development).
                                                           2. Develop a portfolio of recommended resilient design standards, such as
                                                              such as water-wise landscaping permeable paving, and other features to
                                                              encourage developers to incorporate sustainable design elements in future
                                                              projects.

 3   City Planner and Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell presented the 2022 Strategic
 4   Plan for Economic Development and the Fiscal Notes document. He explained that the plan was
 5   approved in January 2022. The Fiscal Notes document could be used to determine some of the
 6   fiscal needs ahead of the budget evaluations. He asked the Council Members and Planning
 7   Commissioners to consider where resources are still needed and what projects should be
 8   prioritized.
 9
10   Mr. Crowell overviewed the Transportation and Infrastructure section of the 2022 Strategic Plan
11   for Economic Development. The 14600 South Master Plan would be done in several pieces, which
12   included the trestle east to I-15. That was already funded by the Redevelopment Agenda (“RDA”)
13   and the report would be released soon. City Engineer, Michael Fazio had been the lead on that
14   work. Some of the report delays pertained to how traffic modeling may impact the intersections.
15   The initial goal was to have that portion of the plan completed by February 28, 2022. Mr. Crowell
16   explained that the deadline was missed to ensure that the report had the most accurate information
17   possible.
18
19   There was also a desire to complete the corridor study from the Trestle West to Redwood Road.
20   Mr. Crowell explained that the City applied for a Utah Department of Transportation (“UDOT”)
21   grant for that work. The application was for $200,000, but the City was still waiting to hear what
22   the outcome would be. If the grant application was unsuccessful and the Council felt it was
23   important to complete the project, additional funding sources would need to be considered. He
24   was not certain whether the City would hear back about the grant before budget adoption.
25
26   The Trestle Reconstruction Project was described as an environmental study that was underway.
27   Mr. Crowell explained that the project had been funded and would be completed by May 2023.
28   There was discussion regarding land purchases. Mr. Crowell noted that there could be future right-
29   of-way acquisition costs to consider, but no money was being requested currently.

Notice is hereby given that the Bluffdale City Council will hold a meeting on Wednesday, April 13, 2022, at Bluffdale City Hall,
2222 West 14400 South, Bluffdale, Utah. The meeting will begin at 6:00 PM or as soon thereafter as possible. This meeting
will also be broadcast live to the public at: www.bluffdale.com. The public may comment at the meeting or by emailing
comments to councilmeetingcomment@bluffdale.com by 4:00 PM the day of the meeting. Emailed comments will be
submitted to the City Council but will not be read at the meeting. Notice is further given that access to this meeting by the
City Council may be by electronic means.
In the event the meeting is disrupted in any way that the City in its sole discretion deems inappropriate, the City reserves the
right to immediately remove the individual(s) from the meeting and, if needed, end virtual access to the meeting. Reasons
for removing an individual or ending virtual access to the meeting include but are not limited to the posting of offensive
pictures, remarks, or making offensive statements, disrespectful statements or actions, and any other action deemed
inappropriate.

                                                     1
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                       MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   2.     Discussion/Presentation Items:
 2
 3          2.1     Discussion Relating to the Plans for The Rose Garden, a Mixed-Use Project,
 4                  Located at the Northwest Corner of 14600 South and PRB near The Point.
 5                  (STACK Real Estate).
 6
 7   Ryan Thomas from STACK Real Estate reported that he is a South Jordan resident. He displayed
 8   and overviewed plans related to The Rose Garden; a 22-acre site located on the northwest corner
 9   of 14600 South. It was the east entrance to Bluffdale and the future front door to The Point Master
10   Planned Development (“MPD”). The site was currently zoned RC (Regional Commercial), but he
11   wanted to discuss a possible change to the SD-X Zone. The Point was in the process of selecting
12   a Master Developer with demolition slated to begin in the fall. Mr. Thomas shared a map and
13   stated that directly north of The Rose Garden site, The Point had plans to include high-density
14   multi-family projects. It made sense that there be a continuation of that type of development. He
15   explained that an Economic Impact Study was conducted that showed that this was the highest and
16   best use from an economic standpoint.
17
18   Mr. Thomas believed that The Point MPD would set the standard in terms of appearance and
19   quality. STACK Real Estate prepared a high-level Conceptual Site Plan to indicate what the layout
20   for The Rose Garden could look like. The site included 1,077 multi-family units. There was a
21   wrap configuration, where the residential units wrapped around the parking structure. This would
22   screen the parking garage from anyone passing by and create a more attractive appearance. After
23   speaking to members of staff, it was determined that a commercial component was important to
24   the City. On the lower right side of the site, a commercial section with 23,000 square feet was
25   planned. Mr. Thomas explained that this was still a high-level plan, and he was not certain whether
26   that would be retail or offices. He was open to any feedback about that. Ultimately, he wanted
27   the commercial space to be prominent and complementary to Bluffdale.
28
29   Council Member Kallas asked for additional information about the desire to rezone. He was
30   hesitant to rezone the property to allow a lot of high-density, multi-unit housing. It was the
31   entrance to the City and the property bordered The Point. He would prefer to keep the RC
32   (Regional Commercial) Zone rather than change it to something else, as the SD-X Zone would


                                                     2
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   result in a significant shift in use. He was not opposed to housing being a component of the
 2   development, but he did feel that the regional commercial feel was necessary.
 3
 4   Mr. Thomas asked what the Council envisioned in the area. Council Member Kallas knew that
 5   The Point was looking at more intense land use in terms of densities and heights. Very little square
 6   footage of commercial had been built to accommodate those units. For the 14600 South area, his
 7   vision was more commercial. Mr. Thomas appreciated the comments shared by Council Member
 8   Kallas. He noted that the challenge with doing commercial was everything that would happen
 9   with The Point. In the first phase of The Point, there would be 190,000 square feet of retail space.
10   To compete with that would be extremely challenging. He did not believe the City wanted to see
11   a shopping center without a lot of traffic. Based on the different concepts STACK Real Estate had
12   looked at, they did not believe that general commercial was a viable use in the area.
13
14   The intention was to include an appropriate amount of parking for the development. Three-
15   bedroom units would be excluded for that reason. There would be a mixture of studios, one-
16   bedroom, and two-bedroom units. The average unit size would be in the range of 700 to 800 square
17   feet. Council Member Kallas explained that he had a different perspective on that area of the City.
18   He believed the development would be the gateway to The Point rather than the tail end of it. This
19   would likely be the busiest intersection in the City, and it would likely be a good place to shop.
20
21   Council Member Aston agreed with many of the comments shared by Council Member Kallas but
22   noted that she did not want to see any housing there. If there was a housing component, it should
23   be a small fraction. Council Member Hales believed what had been proposed was 90% residential
24   and 10% commercial. He suggested that be flipped.
25
26   Council Member Gaston agreed with his fellow Council Members. However, he did not feel
27   residential was necessary and thought that an office complex would succeed in the area with
28   commercial retail through the front. Mr. Thomas explained that certain challenges had been
29   identified. For instance, putting office uses in The Rose Garden would be difficult because office
30   uses would also be a part of The Point development. It would be hard to compete from a leasing



                                                      3
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                       MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   standpoint. That combined with the lack of available housing had driven the decisions. He
 2   wondered if there was a middle ground between housing and commercial that could be envisioned.
 3   Council Member Kallas was open to hearing innovative and interesting ideas.
 4
 5   Mr. Thomas reported that STACK Real Estate would consider doing ground floor retail with multi-
 6   family above it. He asked if that was a more attractive concept to the Council. Council Member
 7   Kallas explained that the Council was looking at the area as a commercial district. The housing
 8   component should be secondary. Council Member Crockett believed there would be 7,500
 9   residential units at The Point. 10,000 residents were expected to be there. The City was also in
10   the process of looking at 14600 South east of the trestle and doing some road alignments with
11   Draper City. She did not know what that would look like. Mr. Thomas reported that their density
12   numbers were in line with what is north of the property. The Rose Garden would be a continuation.
13   Council Member Crockett was concerned about too much density in one area. She also felt it was
14   important to know what the road alignment would be.
15
16   Nathan Ricks of STACK Real Estate explained that when the property was purchased over five
17   years ago, it was intended to include office space. After The Point project was announced, it
18   changed the nature of their investment. STACK Real Estate had to completely rethink the site and
19   what would make the most sense in the area. If commercial is important to the City from an
20   economic point of view, there would be more property tax from the proposed development as
21   compared to a superstore. The per acre property tax return would be significantly higher within
22   the development. As for sales tax, the number one remitter of sales tax in Lehi, Utah, was Amazon
23   rather than Costco. It was confirmed that more people shop online. The nature of retail has
24   radically changed. Rather than perpetuate the former model of commercial development that was
25   struggling, it may make sense to shift to something else.
26
27   Mayor Hall understood that economically, the proposed plan would benefit the City. She also
28   understood that retail had changed. However, Bluffdale is a service desert. It was not necessarily
29   about how much money would be brought into the City but whether enough services are being




                                                     4
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   provided for residents. The Council did not seem ready to change the zoning at the current time.
 2   There needed to be improved services in that area.
 3
 4          2.2     Discussion Relating to the Jordan River Boat Ramp Launch Proposal by Salt
 5                  Lake County Parks and Recreation. (Staff Presenters, Jennifer Robison and
 6                  Todd Sheeran).
 7
 8   Senior City Planner, Jennifer Robison reported that within the last year there have been meetings
 9   with staff from Salt Lake County Parks and Recreation. There was a desire to use County funds
10   to install a boat ramp launch in Bluffdale on the Jordan River. She felt this would be an asset to
11   the parks and trails system. Mrs. Robison displayed and overviewed a PowerPoint presentation
12   related to the Jordan River Water Trail boat launch at Parry Farms Park.
13
14   Mrs. Robison reported that the Jordan River runs approximately 5.58 miles in Bluffdale. Coming
15   from Utah Lake, there were three major conflicts, including the Turner Dam, where the river
16   diverts into several canals, as well as the upper and lower Jordan Narrows diversion structures.
17   The proposed boat launch would be at 15000 South in Parry Farms. She discussed the proposed
18   boat launch location and identified on a map displayed. It was difficult to find areas along the river
19   without conflicts. For instance, many areas had conflicts with Rocky Mountain Power and sewers.
20   The County wanted to look at putting in a parking area and a trail that led towards the boat ramp.
21
22   On the plat for Parry Farms, there was private driveway access that went back into the homes to
23   the north. That easement would remain and would not interfere with resident access. The plans
24   were included in both the packet and the PowerPoint presentation. Mrs. Robison reported that the
25   proposal was to put in a parking lot that would allow for drop-off access. There would also be a
26   sidewalk that would connect to the City sidewalk and allow visitors to get from the parking lot to
27   the boat launch. There was a meeting with the County and the South Jordan Canal Company. The
28   South Jordan Canal Company was in support of the County utilizing a portion of their service road.
29
30   City Engineer, Michael Fazio reported that many sites were considered, but Staff felt this location
31   was ideal. Council Member Hales wondered if traffic would be increased. Mr. Fazio noted that
32   there would be some traffic but there would not be a drastic increase. Council Member Kallas


                                                       5
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   liked the idea of the boat ramp but noted that it might be difficult to carry a kayak down due to the
 2   elevation. Mr. Fazio agreed with him but explained that the options were limited. It may be
 3   possible to provide carts to make it easier to move everything down. Council Member Crockett
 4   suggested there be a drop zone closer to the river with parking up higher. City Manager, Mark
 5   Reid, believed there could be a hammerhead access. Mr. Fazio stated that the City could ask the
 6   County to look into that further and determine whether there could be a drop-off spot.
 7
 8   Council Member Kallas noted that a power source in the area would be preferable, which would
 9   allow inflatable rafts to be used. There was further discussion regarding access. Council Member
10   Aston explained that she was part of the Jordan River Commission. She was excited about the
11   proposal. While the location has challenges due to elevation changes, it was a good location
12   overall. Mr. Reid noted that the City wants to see the ownership of the property go to Salt Lake
13   County so that it is not under Bluffdale’s name. That means the County would assume all liability,
14   maintenance, and care. There had been discussions about possible land swaps.
15
16          2.3     Discussion Relating to Leasing Property on Crooked Sky. (Staff Presenter,
17                  Mark Reid).
18
19   Mr. Reid displayed and overviewed the proposed surplus property at the corner of Iron Horse
20   Boulevard and Crooked Sky Drive. He explained that coming down Iron Horse Boulevard, there
21   are four homes. The first turn-off to the right was Crooked Sky Drive. The home on the corner
22   has 30 to 40 feet behind it that is collecting junk. For instance, there is a sewer pipe and various
23   tree stumps. This was City open space. The homeowner wants to buy or lease that piece of land.
24   Mr. Reid shared a map with the Council. The proposal from Staff was to either lease or sell the
25   0.20 acres to the homeowner. Mr. Reid believed it would be beneficial to the City to incorporate
26   the land into the existing property.
27
28   Mr. Reid asked the Council Members to think about what to do with similar open space areas that
29   have no use to the City. No decision needed to be made tonight and the item would be brought
30   back for further discussion at a future City Council Work Session. Council Member Crockett
31   wanted to see the surplus area in person to better understand the elevations. Mayor Hall asked that


                                                      6
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                       BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                          MEETING MINUTES
                                         Wednesday, April 13, 2022

 1   it be added to the Work Session for the next meeting. Mr. Reid noted that this would lead to a
 2   larger discussion about whether the Council feels it is appropriate to lease or sell similar types of
 3   lands. Council Member Kallas felt that might be a more case-by-case basis because it depends on
4    whether someone wants the land. City Attorney, Todd Sheeran believed that if the Council decides
5    to move forward with the surplus property, there would likely be a Lease Agreement similar to
6    what has been done in the past.
 7

12
13          7.1     Ordinance Number 2022-07 – Amending Bluffdale City Code, Sections
14                  11.20.020, 11.350.020, 11.350.030, and 11.160.160 of the Bluffdale City Land
15                  Use Ordinances to Update the Standards Pertaining to Public Utility
16                  Substations, City Initiated, Application 2022-06. (Staff Presenter, Ellen
17                  Oakman).
18
19   Associate City Planner, Ellen Oakman reported that the above item pertains to the Public Utility
20   Substation Ordinance. The Planning Commission recommended that the amendments be approved
21   but wanted to leave out any mention of cell towers. Ms. Oakman shared background information.
22   The GC-1 (General Commercial) Zone is not ideal for Public Utility Substations due to the limited
23   commercial frontage and the desire for more viable commercial uses and aesthetically pleasing
24   streetscapes. As a result, Public Utility Substations were removed from the GC-1 (General
25   Commercial) Zone. Ms. Oakman shared a zoning map with the Council.
26
27   The proposed amendments would add Public Utility Substations as a definition in 11.20.020.
28   There would also be updates to 11.160.160 – Public Utility Substations related to design and
29   landscape standards. That section previously stated that it was a conditional use. It was proposed
30   to be permitted with the approval of the Zoning Administrator. Additionally, the Land Use Tables
31   in 11.350.020 and 11.250.030 were updated. For landscaping, there was a proposed setback of a
32   minimum of 10 feet with medium-sized trees. Water efficiency standards must be met. There was
33   also information about a perimeter wall, which had an eight-foot minimum and a 12-foot
34   maximum.

              REQUEST FOR CITY COUNCIL ACTION

To:                             Mayor and City Council
From:                           Jennifer Robison, Senior Planner
Date:                           May 4, 2022
Business Date:                  May 11, 2022
Subject:                        Proposed ordinance text amendments to Bluffdale City Codes
                                11.11.070.030 and 11.11.070.090 (Mixed Use Zone) to allow a “property
                                management sales/leasing office” in a residential area in the Mixed Use
                                zoning district (MU) as an Accessory Use.
Applicant(s):                   Banner Hill LLC
Staff Presentation:             Jennifer Robison

RECOMMENDATION: To approve the Text Amendment Application (2022-10) as recommended by the
Planning Commission and direction from City Council to add text for the “sales/leasing office not to exceed 500
square feet.”

BACKGROUND: The Council conducted and closed the public hearing regarding this application. The
application was tabled to add some additional language to address the size of a future building as an
accessory use to a multi-family project. The draft ordinance is attached for the Council to consider with the
text amendments highlighted in GREEN.

FINDINGS FOR APPROVAL:
The Planning Commission recommendation for approval of the Text Amendment Application are based on the
following findings:
1.    That the proposed text amendments meet the intent of the current Mixed Use (MU) Zone and
      Independence at the Point Project Plan.
2.    That all requirements of the City Codes, adopted ordinances and the Independence at the Point Project
      Plan will be met and adhered to for all future applications.
3.    That the text amendment to allow a “property management sales/leasing office” as an accessory use to
      allowed multi-family uses and is subject to and shall comply with all site plan requirements.
4.    That all construction drawings be approved and stamped by the City Engineer prior to any construction
      of City improvements, if applicable.
5.    That all proposed buildings are subject to all building permit requirements.

                                   ORDINANCE 2022-08

  AN ORDINANCE OF THE CITY COUNCIL OF THE CITY OF BLUFFDALE,
 UTAH, AMENDING BLUFFDALE CITY CODE 11.11.070.030 (DEFINITIONS IN
 MIXED USE ZONE), 11.11.070.090 (MIXED USE LAND USE REQUIREMENTS)

        WHEREAS, Utah Code 10-9a-102 grants Bluffdale City (the "City") authority to
enact ordinances that the Bluffdale City Council (the "City Council") considered necessary or
appropriate for the use and development of land within the City; and

       WHEREAS, the subject text amendment modifies the Mixed Use Zone by allowing
Property Management Sales/Leasing Offices as an Accessory Use in the Mixed Use
Residential Land Use Requirements in the Mixed Use Zone section and Definitions chapter;
and

      WHEREAS, the Planning Commission held a public hearing and gave the City
Council its recommendation; and

        WHEREAS, after holding a public hearing and reviewing the subject text amendment,
the City Council finds that the subject text amendment will enhance public health, safety, and
welfare.

       NOW THEREFORE, be it ordained by the Council of the Bluffdale City, in the State
of Utah, as follows:


      SECTION 1:           AMENDMENT “11.11.070.030 Definitions” of the Bluffdale
Municipal Code is hereby amended as follows:


                                      AMEN DMEN T

11.11.070.030 Definitions

For the purposes of this article, the following terms shall have the meanings described:

BUILDING HEIGHT OR HEIGHT: The vertical distance as measured continuously along a
line at existing grade bisecting the width of the lot to the highest point of a building or
structure.

BUSINESS SERVICES: An establishment completely operating within a building, providing
other businesses with various services including maintenance, repair and service, testing,
rental, etc.; also includes business equipment repair services (except vehicle repair) computer
related services (rental, repair), equipment rental businesses within buildings, film processing
and photofinishing, janitorial and window cleaning services, mailbox services and similar uses.




                                             Page 1
COMMERCIAL RECREATION, INDOOR: A commercial recreational land use conducted
entirely within a building, including arcade, arena, athletic and health clubs, bowling alley,
community center, gymnasium, pool or billiard hall, skating rink, swimming pool, tennis court
or similar indoor facility. Related indoor uses may include a retail area for the sale or rental of
equipment and a snack bar.

DIGITAL PRINTING AND PUBLISHING: A printing establishment providing convenience
photocopying and accessory retail oriented services.

FLOOR AREA RATIO OR FAR: The numerical value obtained through dividing the gross
floor area of a building, or buildings, located on a lot by the total area of such lot. Any area
used exclusively for vehicle parking and loading, enclosed vertical shafts or elevators shall not
be included in determining floor area ratio.

FURNITURE AND HOME FURNISHING SERVICES: Retailers of new furniture and
home furnishings from fixed point of sale locations using showrooms and similar areas for the
presentation of their products. Many offer interior decorating services in addition to the sale of
products.

HARDWARE AND VARIETY STORES: A retail store that sells or rents tools, fasteners, and
similar objects known collectively as hardware, not including lumber, as well as related
sundries used in construction and repair.

HOME HEALTHCARE EQUIPMENT AND SERVICES: A business that sells or rents
home healthcare equipment that provides therapeutic benefits or enables the beneficiary to
perform tasks that he or she is unable to undertake including, but not limited to, equipment
such as wheelchairs, hospital beds, traction equipment, walkers, kidney machines, oxygen,
canes, crutches and other medically needed items.

INDUSTRIAL PARK: A master planned industrial complex typically in a suburban area and
set in parklike surroundings.

LIVE/WORK UNIT: A dwelling unit that includes ground floor space for a business that is
operated by the individuals living in the upstairs residential unit.

LOT: As defined by the city of Bluffdale subdivision ordinance.

MIXED USE LOT: A combination of both residential and nonresidential uses located on a lot
of record, as recorded at the office of the county recorder.

MIXED USE STRUCTURE: A combination of both residential and nonresidential uses
located within the same building.

MIXED USES: See definition of Mixed Use Lot or Mixed Use Structure.




                                               Page 2
OPEN SPACE: Land located within the area of the project plan and which is not individually
owned by residents, or some other form of individual ownership, and which is provided in
perpetuity for the protection, preservation or conservation of environmentally sensitive areas or
for the use and enjoyment of owners within the project plan area or residents of the city as a
whole, and provided as required in this article.

PERMITTED DENSITY: The allowed residential density, as identified in section 11-11G-12,
table 2 of this article plus any applicable open space incentive density benefits (section 11-11G-
18, table 3 of this article) and/or any discretionary residential project design and amenity
benefits (section 11-11G-15 of this article).

PERSONAL IMPROVEMENT ESTABLISHMENT: A business that provides instructional
services or facilities, including health or physical fitness clubs, modeling agencies, rehearsal
halls, and weight control clinics.

PERSONAL SERVICE ESTABLISHMENT: An establishment providing nonmedical
services to individuals as a primary use. Examples of these uses include barbershops, beauty
salons, day/health spa, hair salons, nail salons, shoe repair shops, tanning salons, tailors and
similar businesses not including tattoo establishments.

PROFESSIONAL SERVICES: Services defined as infrequent, technical, and/or unique
functions performed by independent contractors/consultants who may or may not require
licensing. Such professional services include, but are not limited to, accounting and billing
services, appraisal services, artists, graphic design, consulting services, data processing
assessments, environmental studies, financial and operational audits, legal services,
management information systems studies, property management services ,including
sales/leasing office, surveys and feasibility studies, technology implementation services,
training services, translation services and similar professional services.

SENSITIVE AREAS OR LANDS:

     A. Wetlands, as identified by the U.S. army corps of engineers, or a wetlands delineation,
        as provided by a certified wetlands delineator;
     B. Floodplains and floodways, as identified by the latest FIRM maps, as provided by the
        federal emergency management agency;
     C. Steep slopes, areas of slope at or exceeding a thirty percent (30%) grade or three to
        one (3:1) run to rise ratio; and
     D. Fault lines, stream corridors and other areas encumbered by geological hazards.

SMALL ANIMAL VETERINARY CLINIC: A business operated by licensed animal health
professionals that are qualified to diagnose and treat a variety of companion species such as
dogs, cats, other small mammals, birds, and reptiles.

STORY: As defined in the currently adopted building code of the city. (Ord. 2013-20, 12-17-
2013)

            REQUEST FOR CITY COUNCIL ACTION

To:                            Mayor and City Council
From:                          Grant Crowell
Date:                          May 3, 2022
Business Date:                 May 11, 2022
Subject:                       City Initiated Text Amendment Regarding Commercial Trucks in
                               Residential Zones and Related Ordinance Provisions
Staff Presentation:            Grant Crowell


RECOMMENDATION:
To approve the proposed ordinance amendments to Bluffdale City Code sections 3.80 (Home Occupations),
6.20 (Truck Routes), 11.20 and 11.160 (Land Use Ordinance) to update the standards related to truck routes,
storage, use and parking of large commercial vehicles in certain residential zones, as recommended by the
Planning Commission.

SUMMARY:
Due to community and City Council concern over increased semi-truck traffic and alleged trucking operations
being operated out of properties in the R-1-43 zone, Staff was requested to review options to address these
concerns. Staff held several work sessions with the City Council regarding this topic which covered a
spectrum of options. These options ranged from restrictive to laissez faire and included elements of the land
use ordinance, the business license code, and the truck routes ordinance and map.

After arriving at general consensus on some key provisions, Staff was directed to move ordinance amendment
proposals through the Planning Commission’s review and hearing process to gain their feedback. The
ordinance proposed amends sections of the truck route ordinance to clarify issues that have made
prosecution of offenses unsuccessful and to update the routes map; to make the major home occupation
maximum truck size be up to 26,000 Gross Vehicle Weight Rating (GVWR); and to very specifically address the
types of commercial trucks that can be stored in the R-1-43, R-1-87, and A-5 zones.

During the Planning Commission review process, several changes were made to the original ordinance which
would still allow one take home large commercial truck and trailer in residential areas which are not on a
truck route and to remove the screening and setback requirements, among other clarifications in the final
recommended ordinance.
